Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

Print the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Examples

Input

3
28800 57600
28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2


Input

10
28800 57600
4800 9600
6000 1200
600 600
300 600
5400 600
6000 5760
6760 2880
6000 12000
9000 600


Output

5

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Takahashi has a record of N sleep-awake cycles: (a₁,b₁), (a₂,b₂), ..., (aₙ,bₙ)
2. Each cycle is: sleep for aᵢ seconds, then awake for bᵢ seconds
3. We need to find the maximum possible number of times he woke up early (between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM inclusive)
4. The day has 86400 seconds (24 hours)
5. The early wake-up period is 10800 seconds (3 hours from 4:00 to 7:00 AM)

## Key Insights

1. We don't know the starting time T, so we can choose T optimally to maximize early wake-ups
2. Each wake-up happens at time T + sum of all previous sleep times + the "start of this cycle"
3. For a wake-up to be early, its time must be in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM] = [10800, 11880] seconds from a reference point (or mod 86400)
4. Since we can choose T freely, we can shift the entire timeline to maximize early wake-ups
5. A wake-up at time t happens at time (t mod 86400) in the day cycle

## Strategy

1. Calculate the exact time of each wake-up relative to start time T
2. For each wake-up i, its time is: T + sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(a_j) + a_i
3. We want this time to fall in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM] = [10800, 11880] mod 86400
4. This gives us a condition on T: the wake-up time (T + offset_i) mod 86400 ∈ [10800, 11880]
5. We can transform this to: T mod 86400 ∈ [10800 - offset_i, 11880 - offset_i] mod 86400
6. We want to maximize the number of wake-ups that satisfy this condition
7. We can use a sweep line approach to find the optimal T

## Simplified Approach

Since we can choose T optimally, we only need to check when the wake-up times fall in the early period. For each wake-up i, we can determine the range of T values that make it early.

The wake-up time for cycle i is: T + sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(a_j) + a_i

We want: (T + offset_i) mod 86400 ∈ [10800, 11880]

This means: T mod 86400 ∈ [10800 - offset_i, 11880 - offset_i] mod 86400

Actually, simpler: for each wake-up i, if we can make it fall in [10800, 11880], we count it. We want the maximum number of such wake-ups possible due to our choice of T.

We can use a sliding window approach or simply check all possible positions, but since we can shift T freely, we need to find the optimal alignment.

Better approach:
- Calculate the cumulative sleep time before each wake-up
- For each wake-up i, calculate the required T range such that wake-up happens in [10800, 11880]
- Use a sweep to count how many wake-ups can be early simultaneously

Actually, simpler: since we can choose T optimally, we just need to determine when the wake-up times fall within the early period, and count the maximum possible.

Let me implement a direct approach:

```python
def solve():
    N = int(input())
    cycles = []
    total_sleep = 0
    
    for _ in range(N):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        cycles.append((a, b))
        total_sleep += a
    
    # Calculate wake-up times relative to start T
    # Wake-up i happens at time T + sum of previous sleep times + a_i
    # We want this to be in [10800, 11880] mod 86400
    
    # For each wake-up, find the range of T that makes it early
    # Wake-up i occurs at T + pre_i where pre_i = sum of a_j for j < i + a_i
    pre_time = 0
    wake_up_times = []
    
    for i in range(N):
        a, b = cycles[i]
        # Wake-up happens at T + pre_time + a
        # We want (T + pre_time + a) mod 86400 to be in [10800, 11880]
        # This means T mod 86400 must be in [10800 - (pre_time + a), 11880 - (pre_time + a)] mod 86400
        
        if pre_time + a < 0:
            # Handle negative case
            target_start = (10800 - (pre_time + a)) % 86400
            target_end = (11880 - (pre_time + a)) % 86400
        else:
            target_start = 10800 - (pre_time + a)
            target_end = 11880 - (pre_time + a)
            
        # If target_start > target_end, wrap around
        if target_start > target_end:
            target_start = target_start - 86400
        
        wake_up_times.append((target_start, target_end))
        pre_time += a
    
    # Now we need to find if there's a T that makes maximum wake-ups early
    # Since the ranges are continuous, we can find overlaps
    
    # Actually, simpler: we can just count how many wake-ups can fall in the window
    # For each wake-up, it's early if the required T interval overlaps
    # But we want maximum compatibility
    
    # Since we can choose T, we can try to find T that maximizes count
    # But with N up to 10^5, we can't iterate over all T
    
    # Key insight: The maximum number of early wake-ups is bounded by the length of the day
    # We can use a sweep line: for each wake-up, add its interval of T that makes it early
    # Then find maximum coverage
    
    # But the intervals are defined over [0, 86400)
    events = []
    
    for start, end in wake_up_times:
        if start < 0:
            events.append((start + 86400, 1))  # start positive
            events.append((end + 86400, -1))  # end positive
        else:
            events.append((start, 1))
            events.append((end, -1))
    
    events.sort()
    
    max_count = 0
    current_count = 0
    
    for time, delta in events:
        current_count += delta
        max_count = max(max_count, current_count)
    
    print(max_count)

solve()

```python
N = int(input())
cycles = []
total_sleep = 0

for _ in range(N):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    cycles.append((a, b))
    total_sleep += a

# Calculate the offset for each wake-up (time since start)
offsets = []
current_offset = 0

for a, b in cycles:
    # Wake-up happens at current_offset + a seconds after start time
    offsets.append(current_offset + a)
    current_offset += a

# For each wake-up, find T range that makes it early
events = []

for offset in offsets:
    # We want (T + offset) mod 86400 in [10800, 11880]
    # So T mod 86400 must be in [(10800 - offset) mod 86400, (11880 - offset) mod 86400]
    
    # Calculate the start and end of T interval
    start_t = (10800 - offset) % 86400
    end_t = (11880 - offset) % 86400
    
    # If start_t > end_t, it wraps around
    if start_t > end_t:

        events.append((86400, -1))
        events.append((0, 1))
        events.append((end_t, -1))
    else:
        events.append((start_t, 1))
        events.append((end_t, -1))

# Sort events by time
events.sort()

max_count = 0
current_count = 0

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Takahashi has a daily pattern recorded as: `a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ..., a_N, b_N`
2. The pattern is: sleep for `a_i` seconds, then awake for `b_i` seconds
3. He wakes up N times (once after each `a_i` period)
4. We need to find the maximum number of times he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM (inclusive)
5. A day has 86400 seconds, and 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds
6. We don't know the starting time T, so we need to find the maximum possible count

## Key Insights

1. We can shift the entire timeline by any amount T (the starting time)
2. Each waking up event occurs at time `T + sum of all previous a_i + some offset`
3. For each wake-up event i, it happens at time: `T + sum(a_1 to a_{i-1})`
4. We want to maximize the number of wake-up times that fall in the 4:00-7:00 AM window

## Approach

1. For each wake-up event i, the wake-up time is: `T + cumulative_sum_before_i`
2. We want `T + cumulative_sum_before_i` to be in the range `[4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]` which is `[4*3600, 7*3600]` = `[14400, 25200]` in seconds from midnight
3. The condition for wake-up i to happen between 4:00-7:00 AM is:
   - `14400 ≤ T + cumulative_sum_before_i ≤ 25200`
   - This means: `14400 - cumulative_sum_before_i ≤ T ≤ 25200 - cumulative_sum_before_i`
4. We need to find a value of T that maximizes the number of wake-ups in this range
5. For each wake-up event, the valid range of T is `[14400 - cumulative_sum_before_i, 25200 - cumulative_sum_before_i]`
6. We can find the maximum overlap by considering all these intervals

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the cumulative sum of `a_i` values
2. For each wake-up event i, calculate the valid range of T
3. Count how many events can be satisfied for different positions of T
4. The maximum count occurs when we choose T optimally

Actually, a simpler approach: since we want to maximize the count, we can consider that the optimal T will make as many wake-up times as possible fall within the 4:00-7:00 window.

For each wake-up event i:
- It occurs at time: `T + S_i` where `S_i` is the sum of all `a_j` for `j < i`
- We want `14400 ≤ T + S_i ≤ 25200`
- So: `14400 - S_i ≤ T ≤ 25200 - S_i`

The key insight is that we need to find a single T that satisfies the maximum number of these inequalities. This is a classic interval overlap problem.

We can do this by:
1. For each wake-up event i, we have a range `[L_i, R_i]` where `L_i = 14400 - S_i` and `R_i = 25200 - S_i`
2. Count how many events can be satisfied by choosing T optimally

Since we want to maximize the count, we can consider that the best T is one that satisfies the most constraints. We can use a sweep line approach or simply check the endpoints.

Actually, simpler: for each possible wake-up event, we can determine if it can be in the window for different values of T, but since we can choose T, we want to maximize the total.

The optimal strategy is to consider that we want as many wake-up events as possible to fall in the 4:00-7:00 window. Since we can shift T, we can make exactly the events that have their wake-up times in the interval `[14400, 25200]` when we choose T properly.

But we need to account for the cumulative sums.

## Final Algorithm

1. Calculate cumulative sums of `a_i` values
2. For each wake-up event i (0-indexed), the wake-up time is `T + sum of a_1 to a_{i}` (after sleeping a_i)
3. Actually, let me re-read: after sleeping a_1, then awake for b_1 → wake-up at T + a_1
4. So wake-up i happens at T + sum(a_1 to a_i)  
5. For wake-up i to be between 4:00 and 7:00: `14400 ≤ T + sum(a_1 to a_i) ≤ 25200`
6. So `14400 - sum(a_1 to a_i) ≤ T ≤ 25200 - sum(a_1 to a_i)`

We can now just find the maximum number of intervals that overlap at any point.

```python
n = int(input())
a_b = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    a_b.append((a, b))

# Calculate cumulative sum of a_i
cumulative_sum = 0
intervals = []

for i in range(n):
    a, b = a_b[i]
    # Wake-up at T + cumulative_sum (after sleeping a_i)
    wake_up_time = cumulative_sum + a  # This is wrong
    
    # Let me reconsider:
    # After a_1: wake-up at T + a_1
    # After a_2: wake-up at T + a_1 + a_2
    # So after a_i: wake-up at T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i)
    
    cumulative_sum += a
    intervals.append((14400 - cumulative_sum, 25200 - cumulative_sum))

# Count maximum overlap
# We need to find the maximum number of intervals that contain any common point
# We can do this by checking all endpoints and counting overlaps

events = []
for start, end in intervals:
    events.append((start, 1))  # start event
    events.append((end, -1))  # end event

events.sort()

max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)

Ah! I see! I misunderstood. The wake-up happens after sleeping, not after just the sleep periods.

The pattern is:
- Sleep for a_1 (T to T+a_1)
- Awake for b_1 (T+a_1 to T+a_1+b_1)
- Sleep for a_2 (T+a_1+b_1 to T+a_1+b_1+a_2)
- Awake for b_2 (T+a_1+b_1+a_2 to T+a_1+b_1+a_2+b_2)
- ...

So the wake-up times are:
- After sleep 1: T + a_1
- After sleep 2: T + a_1 + b_1 + a_2
- After sleep 3: T + a_1 + b_1 + a_2 + b_2 + a_3
- ...

So the wake-up time for wake-up i is: T + (a_1 + b_1 + ... + b_{i-1} + a_i)

For i=1: wake-up at T + a_1
For i=2: wake-up at T + a_1 + b_1 + a_2
For i=3: wake-up at T + a_1 + b_1 + a_2 + b_2 + a_3

So the cumulative sum for wake-up i is the sum of a_j for j=1 to i plus the sum of b_j for j=1 to i-1.

Let me implement this:

```python
n = int(input())
# Read the input
pairs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    pairs.append((a, b))

# Calculate wake-up times in terms of T
# For wake-up i (1-indexed), time = T + sum of (a_j for j=1 to i) + sum of (b_j for j=1 to i-1)

# Calculate cumulative sums
a_cumulative = []
b_cumulative = []

# b_cumulative[i] = b_1 + b_2 + ... + b_i

# For i=2: T + a_cumulative[2] + b_cumulative[1] = T + (a_1+a_2+a_3) + (b_1+b_2)

intervals = []

# For each wake-up i (0-indexed)
for i in range(n):
    if i == 0:
        # Wake-up after first sleep
        total_time = a_cumulative[1]  # This is wrong
        pass

# I need to recalculate
# For wake-up i (0-indexed), wake-up time = T + (sum of a from 1 to i+1) + (sum of b from 1 to i)
# For i=0: T + a_1
# For i=1: T + (a_1+a_2) + b_1  
# For i=2: T + (a_1+a_2+a_3) + (b_1+b_2)

# So total_offset[i] = sum(a[0] to a[i]) + sum(b[0] to b[i-1]) for i >= 1, and just a[0] for i=0

total_offset = []
for i in range(n):
    if i == 0:
        total_offset.append(pairs[0][0])  # just a_1
    else:
        # sum of a[0] to a[i]
        sum_a = a_cumulative[i+1]
        # sum of b[0] to b[i-1]
        sum_b = b_cumulative[i]
        total_offset.append(sum_a + sum_b)

# Now for each wake-up i, we want T + total_offset[i] to be in [14400, 25200]
# So T in [14400 - total_offset[i], 25200 - total_offset[i]]

intervals = []
for offset in total_offset:
    start = 14400 - offset
    end = 25200 - offset
    intervals.append((start, end))

# Count maximum overlap
events = []
for start, end in intervals:
    events.append((start, 1))
    events.append((end, -1))

events.sort()
max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)
```

I think the problem means that the wake-up happens when he gets up from sleep, which is at the end of each sleep period.

But maybe the time period is modulo 86400. So if a time is negative or greater than 86400, we take modulo 86400.

That makes sense! The day is cyclic, so times are modulo 86400 seconds.

So the wake-up time for event i is (T + offset) mod 86400.

We want (T + offset) mod 86400 to be in [14400, 25200].

This is more complex because we have to consider modulo arithmetic.

For any offset, we want (T + offset) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200].

This means either:
1. T + offset ∈ [14400, 25200] + k * 86400 for some integer k
2. For each wake-up, we have a range of T values that work.

For a given offset, the values of T that make (T + offset) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200] are:
T ∈ [14400 - offset - 86400*k, 25200 - offset - 86400*k] for any integer k

We want to find a T that maximizes the number of these events.

Since the day is 86400 seconds, and we're looking for any k, we need to consider multiple cycles.

But the range of offset can be large, and we need to find the optimal T modulo 86400.

Since the window [14400, 25200] is only 10800 seconds long, and we have periods of up to 10^5 seconds, the offset can be large.

For each wake-up event with offset o, the valid T values are:
T ∈ [14400 - o - 86400*k, 25200 - o - 86400*k] for some k

We want to maximize the number of events that have valid T.

Since the offset values can be large, we need to consider which k values give us the overlap.

For each event, we can consider multiple k values, but we only need to consider k such that the interval [14400 - o - 86400*k, 25200 - o - 86400*k] overlaps with the range of T.

In practice, since the window is 10800 seconds, and the day is 86400 seconds, for each event, there will be at most one k that gives a valid interval that might overlap with others.

Actually, the optimal T will be in [0, 86400) because the pattern repeats every day.

So we can consider T in [0, 86400), and for each wake-up event, determine if (T + offset) mod 86400 is in [14400, 25200].

For a given T in [0, 86400), we can compute whether (T + offset) mod 86400 is in [14400, 25200].

But we want to maximize this over T.

The function is piecewise linear, and the maximum will occur at a boundary or when (T + offset) mod 86400 = 14400 or 25200 for some event.

Since we want the maximum number, we can try all critical points.

The critical points are when T + offset ≡ 14400 (mod 86400) or T + offset ≡ 25200 (mod 86400).

For each event, this gives us T ≡ 14400 - offset (mod 86400) or T ≡ 25200 - offset (mod 86400).

So we have up to 2N critical points in [0, 86400).

We can evaluate the count at each critical point and also at the boundaries.

But since the function is piecewise constant, we only need to check the critical points.

```python
n = int(input())
pairs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    pairs.append((a, b))

# For each wake-up event i (0-indexed):
# wake-up time = T + (sum of a from 0 to i) + (sum of b from 0 to i-1) mod 86400
# When i=0: wake-up at T + a_0
# When i=1: wake-up at T + a_0 + b_0 + a_1
# When i=2: wake-up at T + a_0 + b_0 + a_1 + b_1 + a_2

a_cumulative = [0]
b_cumulative = [0]

for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    a_cumulative.append(a_cumulative[-1] + a)
    b_cumulative.append(b_cumulative[-1] + b)

# The offset for wake-up i is:
# For i=0: offset = a_cumulative[1] - a_cumulative[0] wait no
# For wake-up i: offset = sum of a[0] to a[i] + sum of b[0] to b[i-1]

offsets = []

for i in range(n):
    if i == 0:
        offset = pairs[0][0]  # a_1
    else:
        sum_a = a_cumulative[i+1]  # sum of a[0] to a[i]
        sum_b = b_cumulative[i-1]  # sum of b[0] to b[i-1]
        offset = sum_a + sum_b
    
    offsets.append(offset)

# Critical points for T where (T + offset) mod 86400 = 14400 or 25200
# T = (14400 - offset) mod 86400 or T = (25200 - offset) mod 86400
critical_points = set()

for offset in offsets:
    # T = (14400 - offset) mod 86400
    t1 = (14400 - offset) % 86400
    t2 = (25200 - offset) % 86400
    critical_points.add(t1)
    critical_points.add(t2)

# Also include the boundaries if needed
critical_points.add(0)
critical_points.add(86400)

# Convert to sorted list
critical_points = sorted(critical_points)

# Evaluate at each critical point
max_count = 0

for t in critical_points:
    if t >= 86400:
        t = t % 86400
    
    count = 0
    for offset in offsets:
        wake_up_time = (t + offset) % 86400
        if 14400 <= wake_up_time <= 25200:
            count += 1
    
    max_count = max(max_count, count)

print(max_count)
```

But this might be inefficient if we have many critical points. The number of critical points is at most 2N, which is 200,000, and we have N=100,000, so 2*100,000 = 200,000 critical points, and for each we do N operations, so 200,000 * 100,000 = 20e9 operations which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

Let me think differently. For each wake-up event with offset o, the condition is (T + o) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200].

This is equivalent to T mod 86400 ∈ [14400 - o, 25200 - o] mod 86400.

So T is in an interval modulo 86400.

We want to find the T modulo 86400 that maximizes the number of such intervals.

This is a classic interval covering problem on a circle.

We can do this with a sweep line over the circle.

The events are:
- For each offset o, we have an interval [14400 - o, 25200 - o] mod 86400
- We want to find the point on the circle that is covered by the maximum number of intervals

We can represent the intervals as (start, end) where start and end are in [0, 86400).

For each offset o:
- low = (14400 - o) % 86400
- high = (25200 - o) % 86400
- If low <= high, we have a normal interval [low, high]
- If low > high, we have two intervals [low, 86400) and [0, high]

We can use a sweep line over the circle.

Convert to events:
- For each interval, add events at the start (+1) and end (-1)
- The end event is at high, and for the wrap-around, at 0 for [low, 86400)

But we need to handle the wrap-around.

Here's the efficient approach:

```python
n = int(input())
pairs = []
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    pairs.append((a, b))

# Calculate the offset for each wake-up event
# offset[i] = sum_{j=0}^{i} a_j + sum_{j=0}^{i-1} b_j
a_cumulative = [0]
b_cumulative = [0]

for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    a_cumulative.append(a_cumulative[-1] + a)
    b_cumulative.append(b_cumulative[-1] + b)

offsets = []
for i in range(n):
    if i == 0:
        offset = pairs[0][0]
    else:
        sum_a = a_cumulative[i]  # a_0 to a_i
        sum_b = b_cumulative[i-1]  # b_0 to b_{i-1}
        offset = sum_a + sum_b
    offsets.append(offset)

# We want to find T such that (T + offset) mod 86400 in [14400, 25200]
# This means T in [14400 - offset, 25200 - offset] mod 86400

events = []  # (time, delta)

for offset in offsets:
    low = (14400 - offset) % 86400
    high = (25200 - offset) % 86400
    
    # If low <= high, it's a single interval
    if low <= high:
        events.append((low, 1))
        events.append((high, -1))
    else:
        # low > high, so we have [low, 86400) and [0, high]
        events.append((low, 1))
        events.append((86400, -1))  # at 86400, which is 0
        events.append((0, 1))
        events.append((high, -1))

# Sort events by time
events.sort()

max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

Input

N
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

Print the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

3
28800 57600
28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N sleep-awake cycles: (a₁, b₁), (a₂, b₂), ..., (aₙ, bₙ)
2. Each cycle means: sleep for aᵢ seconds, then awake for bᵢ seconds
3. We need to find how many times Takahashi wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM (inclusive)
4. The key insight: we don't know the starting time T, so we can choose T to maximize the number of early wake-ups
5. We want the maximum possible number of early wake-ups

## Key Observations

1. A day has 86400 seconds (24 hours)
2. The period from 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM is 3 hours = 10800 seconds
3. We can shift the entire timeline by any amount (mod 86400) to find the optimal starting point
4. For each wake-up time (after sleep period aᵢ), we need to check if it falls in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]

## Approach

1. For each wake-up event (at time T + sum of all aⱼ for j=1 to i), we need to check if it falls in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]
2. Since we can choose T optimally, we can shift the timeline to maximize the count
3. The wake-up times are at positions: T + a₁, T + a₁ + b₁, T + a₁ + b₁ + a₂, T + a₁ + b₁ + a₂ + b₂, etc.
4. We can think of this as: for each wake-up time (which is T + offset), we want it to fall in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the cumulative time offset after each wake-up
2. For a given T, wake-up at time T + offset_i falls in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM] if:
   (T + offset_i) mod 86400 is in [4*3600, 7*3600] = [14400, 25200]
3. We want to maximize the count over all possible T
4. We can use the fact that the wake-up times are periodic and find the optimal T by checking key points

Actually, let me think of this more systematically:

The wake-up times are at: T + sum_{j=1}^i a_j for i from 1 to N

For wake-up i to occur between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, we need:
(T + cumsum_a[i]) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200]

This is equivalent to:
T mod 86400 ∈ [14400 - cumsum_a[i], 25200 - cumsum_a[i]] (mod 86400)

For each wake-up, this defines a range of T values that would make that wake-up occur in early hours.

We want to find T that maximizes the number of wake-ups in this range.

Since we're working modulo 86400, we can think of this as finding the T that maximizes the overlap with the union of these intervals.

The key insight: we only need to check T values at the boundaries of these intervals, or at critical points.

However, there's a simpler approach: for each wake-up i, determine the range of T that makes it fall in early hours, then find the T that maximizes the total count.

But since N can be up to 10⁵, we need an efficient solution.

Alternative approach: since the constraints are on modulo arithmetic, and the wake-up times are determined by cumulative sums, we can:

1. Calculate cumulative sums of a_i
2. For each wake-up i, calculate the required range of T for it to be in early hours

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, the problem is simpler:

For each wake-up i (after a₁, a₁+b₁, a₁+b₁+a₂, etc.), the wake-up time is T + cumsum_a[i].

We want (T + cumsum_a[i]) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200].

This means T mod 86400 ∈ [14400 - cumsum_a[i], 25200 - cumsum_a[i]] (mod 86400).

Each wake-up gives us an interval of T values. We want to find T that maximizes the count of intervals it belongs to.

Since we're working mod 86400, we can represent each interval and find the T that maximizes the overlap.

```python
def solve():
    N = int(input())
    pairs = []
    for _ in range(N):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        pairs.append((a, b))
    
    # Calculate cumulative sleep times
    cumsum_a = []
    cumsum = 0
    for a, b in pairs:
        cumsum += a
        cumsum_a.append(cumsum)
    
    # Target time window: 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM = 10800 seconds
    # In seconds from start of day: 4:00 AM = 4*3600 = 14400, 7:00 AM = 7*3600 = 25200
    start_time = 14400
    end_time = 25200
    day_length = 86400
    
    # For each wake-up event, find the range of T that makes it fall in early hours
    # Wake-up i happens at T + cumsum_a[i]
    # We want (T + cumsum_a[i]) mod day_length in [start_time, end_time]
    # So T mod day_length in [start_time - cumsum_a[i], end_time - cumsum_a[i]] (mod day_length)
    
    intervals = []
    for i in range(N):
        cumsum = cumsum_a[i]
        # The range of T values that make wake-up i occur in early hours
        # T in [start_time - cumsum, end_time - cumsum] mod day_length
        low = start_time - cumsum
        high = end_time - cumsum
        
        # Handle modulo arithmetic
        # If low > high, it means the interval wraps around
        if low > high:
            # Split into two intervals
            intervals.append((low % day_length, day_length))
            intervals.append((0, high % day_length))
        else:
            intervals.append((low, high))
    
    # Count how many intervals contain each point in [0, day_length)
    # But since we're maximizing, we can just check all endpoints or use a sweep line
    # The maximum will occur at one of the endpoints
    
    if not intervals:
        return 0
    
    # Get all critical points (endpoints of intervals)
    points = []
    for low, high in intervals:
        if low < high:
            points.extend([(low, 1), (high, -1)])
        else:
            # wraparound case
            points.extend([(low % day_length, 1), (day_length, -1), (0, 1), (high % day_length, -1)])
    
    # Actually, let's simplify: we just need to check the endpoints
    # The maximum number of overlaps will occur at certain critical points
    
    # Get all unique points where intervals change
    critical_points = set()
    for low, high in intervals:
        if low != high:
            critical_points.add(low % day_length)
            critical_points.add(high % day_length)
            if low < high:
                critical_points.add(low % day_length)
                critical_points.add(high % day_length)
            else:
                # wraparound
                critical_points.add(low % day_length)
                critical_points.add(high % day_length)
    
    # Add endpoints of the day
    critical_points.add(0)
    critical_points.add(day_length)
    
    # Also add some key points
    critical_points.add(start_time % day_length)
    critical_points.add(end_time % day_length)
    
    # Convert to sorted list
    critical_points = sorted(critical_points)
    
    max_count = 0
    for i in range(len(critical_points) - 1):
        t1 = critical_points[i]
        t2 = critical_points[i + 1]
        
        # Count how many intervals cover [t1, t2)
        count = 0
        for low, high in intervals:
            if low < high:
                if t1 >= low and t1 < high:
                    count += 1
                if t1 >= low and t1 == high:
                    count += 1
            else:
                # wraparound
                if t1 >= low and t1 < day_length or t1 >= 0 and t1 < high:
                    count += 1
                if t1 >= low and t1 == high:
                    count += 1
        
        max_count = max(max_count, count)
    
    return max_count

# Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me simplify.

def solve():
    N = int(input())
    pairs = []
    for _ in range(N):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        pairs.append((a, b))
    
    # Calculate cumulative sleep times
    cumsum_a = []
    cumsum = 0
    for a, b in pairs:
        cumsum += a
        cumsum_a.append(cumsum)
    
    # Target time window: 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM = 14400 to 25200 seconds
    start_time = 14400
    end_time = 25200
    day_length = 86400
    
    # For each wake-up event, we need to find T such that
    # (T + cumsum_a[i]) % day_length is in [start_time, end_time]
    # This means T % day_length is in [start_time - cumsum_a[i], end_time - cumsum_a[i]] (mod day_length)
    
    # We'll create intervals of T values that make each wake-up occur in early hours
    # Then we find the T that maximizes the number of intervals it belongs to
    
    intervals = []
    for i in range(N):
        cumsum = cumsum_a[i]
        low = start_time - cumsum
        high = end_time - cumsum
        
        if low < high:
            # Normal interval
            intervals.append((low % day_length, high % day_length))
        else:
            # Wraparound interval
            intervals.append((low % day_length, day_length))
            intervals.append((0, high % day_length))
    
    # Now we need to find T that maximizes the number of intervals it belongs to
    # We can do this by checking all endpoints of intervals
    points = []
    for low, high in intervals:
        points.append(low)
        points.append(high)
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    points = sorted(set(points))
    
    # Also add 0 and day_length for completeness
    points = sorted(set(points + [0, day_length]))
    
    max_count = 0
    for i in range(len(points) - 1):
        t = points[i]
        count = 0
        for low, high in intervals:
            if low <= t < high:
                count += 1
        max_count = max(max_count, count)
    
    # Also check if any point gives more
    for low, high in intervals:
        if low <= 0 < high:
            count = 0
            for l, h in intervals:
                if l <= 0 < h:
                    count += 1
            max_count = max(max_count, count)
    
    return max_count

print(solve())

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

Print the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Examples

28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N pairs (a_i, b_i) representing sleep and wake cycles
2. The pattern is: sleep for a_i seconds, then awake for b_i seconds
3. We need to find the maximum possible number of wake-ups that occur between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM (inclusive)
4. The key insight is that we don't know the starting time T, so we can choose T to maximize the count of wake-ups in the 4-7 AM window

## Key Observations

1. A wake-up occurs at time T + (sum of all sleep durations before that wake-up) + a_i
2. Since we want wake-ups in the 4-7 AM window, we need wake-up times to fall in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]
3. 4:00 AM = 4 * 3600 = 14400 seconds from midnight
4. 7:00 AM = 7 * 3600 = 25200 seconds from midnight
5. So we want wake-up times in [14400, 25200] seconds

6. For each wake-up i (after sleeping for a_1, a_2, ..., a_i), the wake-up time is:
   T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i) + b_i? Wait, no...

Let me reconsider:
- Start at time T
- After a_1 seconds: sleep ends at T + a_1, then awake from T + a_1 to T + a_1 + b_1
- After a_2 seconds: sleep ends at T + a_1 + a_2, then awake from T + a_1 + a_2 to T + a_1 + a_2 + b_2
- After a_i seconds: sleep ends at T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i), then awake from T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i) to T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i) + b_i

So the wake-up time for the i-th wake-up is: T + (a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i)

## Strategy

1. For each wake-up i, calculate the wake-up time as T + prefix_sum[i], where prefix_sum[i] = a_1 + a_2 + ... + a_i
2. We want this time to be in [14400, 25200]
3. So we need: 14400 ≤ T + prefix_sum[i] ≤ 25200
4. Rearranging: 14400 - prefix_sum[i] ≤ T ≤ 25200 - prefix_sum[i]

5. To maximize the count, we need to find a value of T that satisfies the constraints for as many i as possible.

6. For each wake-up i, we have a valid range for T: [14400 - prefix_sum[i], 25200 - prefix_sum[i]]

7. We want to find the maximum number of intervals that can overlap for some value of T.

8. This becomes a classic "maximum overlapping intervals" problem where we need to find the value of T that maximizes the count of valid wake-up times.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate prefix sums of a_i
2. For each wake-up i, determine the valid time range for T
3. Find the T value that maximizes the number of valid wake-ups

The key insight is that the optimal T will be at a boundary point of some interval, or we can use a sweep line approach.

Actually, since we only care about counting, we can:
- For each wake-up i, we have a valid range [L_i, R_i] where L_i = 14400 - prefix_sum[i] and R_i = 25200 - prefix_sum[i]
- We want to find the value of T that lies in the maximum number of these intervals

We can do this by:
1. Creating events: at time L_i, +1; at time R_i, -1
2. Sweep through all possible event points to find maximum overlap

But note: T can be any real value, but since our ranges are discrete, the maximum will occur at one of the endpoints.

## Implementation

```python

    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    pairs.append((a, b))
    prefix_sum += a

for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    # Wake-up time = T + prefix_sum_i
    # Want: 14400 <= T + prefix_sum_i <= 25200
    # So: 14400 - prefix_sum_i <= T <= 25200 - prefix_sum_i

    pass

# Let me recalculate properly
events = []
current_prefix = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    current_prefix += a
    
    # Wake-up time for this occurrence is T + current_prefix
    # Want 14400 <= T + current_prefix <= 25200
    # So T must be in [14400 - current_prefix, 25200 - current_prefix]
    
    events.append((14400 - current_prefix, 1))  # start of valid range
    events.append((25200 - current_prefix, -1)) # end of valid range

# Sort events by time
events.sort()

max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)

So for each wake-up i, we need:

So T ∈ [14400 + 86400*k - prefix_sum[i], 25200 + 86400*k - prefix_sum[i]]

For each i, this gives us a range of T values that work.

```python
n = int(input())
pairs = []
prefix_sum = 0

for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    pairs.append((a, b))
    prefix_sum += a

events = []

# For each wake-up i, we have valid T ranges

    a, b = pairs[i]
    # prefix_sum is cumulative sum up to i
    current_prefix = sum(a for a, _ in pairs[:i+1])  # This is wrong, need to calculate properly
    
    # Recalculate prefix sum for each position
    pass

# Let's recalculate prefix sums correctly
prefix_sums = []
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    current += a
    prefix_sums.append(current)

events = []

for i in range(n):
    prefix_sum_i = prefix_sums[i]
    
    # (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200]
    # So T ∈ [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] + 86400*k for some k
    
    # We need to find all k such that the interval is valid
    # We'll consider k such that the interval is reasonable
    
    # The potential k values range from -k_max to k_max where k_max is large enough
    # Since we're in 24h cycles, we can consider k such that the interval for T is non-empty
    
    # The intervals for T are of the form [14400 - prefix_sum_i - 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i - 86400*k]
    # We need this to be non-empty, so 14400 - prefix_sum_i - 86400*k <= 25200 - prefix_sum_i - 86400*k
    # This is always true, but we need bounds on k
    
    # The interval is non-empty, but we're interested in which k make the interval reasonable
    # For practical purposes, the k value that makes T reasonable is when the interval is within a reasonable range
    
    # Since we're looking for maximum overlap, we can generate events for all reasonable k
    # We can try k values such that the interval is non-empty and doesn't go too far
    
    # But we can't iterate over all k - it could be infinite
    
    # Alternative approach: we want to find T such that (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200]
    # This is equivalent to T ∈ [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] + 86400*Z for some integer Z
    
    # We can generate events at the boundaries for different Z values
    # But Z can be large
    
    # Key insight: T can be chosen in a way that the fractional parts work out
    
    # For each i, the valid T values are in intervals of the form [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k]
    # We need to find T that maximizes the count
    
    # Since the intervals are periodic, we can consider only a finite number of k values
    # The k value that makes the interval most likely to be "in range" is when the interval overlaps with other intervals
    
    # We can generate events for k such that the interval [low, high] has negative or positive values
    
    # Actually, we can consider that the optimal T will be such that the fractional part aligns
    # We can consider T in [0, 86400) and check each wake-up condition
    
    # But N up to 10^5, so we can't check all T values
    
    # Alternative: use sweep line over the boundaries of all valid intervals
    
    # Each valid interval for T: [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k] for some k
    
    # We need to find which k makes sense
    
    # The valid intervals for T are periodic with period 86400
    # We can consider T in [0, 86400) to avoid periodicity issues, and then account for modulo
    
    # For T in [0, 86400), we have (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400 ∈ [14400, 25200]
    # This happens when (T + prefix_sum_i) ∈ [14400, 25200] or (T + prefix_sum_i) ∈ [14400 + 86400, 25200 + 86400] or ...
    
    # But since T >= 0 and T < 86400, T + prefix_sum_i can be large
    
    # So we have two cases:
    # Case 1: T + prefix_sum_i ∈ [14400, 25200] → T ∈ [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] ∩ [0, 86400)
    # Case 2: T + prefix_sum_i ∈ [14400 + 86400, 25200 + 86400] → T ∈ [14400 + 86400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 + 86400 - prefix_sum_i] ∩ [0, 86400)
    # Case 3: T + prefix_sum_i ∈ [14400 + 2*86400, 25200 + 2*86400] → T ∈ [14400 + 2*86400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 + 2*86400 - prefix_sum_i] ∩ [0, 86400)
    # etc.
    
    # But since prefix_sum_i can be large, T + prefix_sum_i might be in [14400 + k*86400, 25200 + k*86400] for some k
    
    # The value of k ranges from -floor(prefix_sum_i/86400) to ceiling((86400-1 - prefix_sum_i)/86400) or something
    
    # This is getting very complex. Let me simplify.
    
    # We can generate all possible valid intervals for T for each wake-up i, with different k values, and then use sweep line.
    
    # For each i, we have valid T values in:
    # For k such that 14400 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400 <= 25200 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400
    # This is always true, but we need the interval to have positive length
    
    # For each i, we need to consider k such that the interval [14400 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400] has positive length
    # Since 25200 - 14400 = 10800 > 0, the length is always positive
    
    # But we only care about T values, and we can consider k in a bounded range.
    
    # The k values that make sense: k_min = ceil(-prefix_sum_i / 86400) and k_max = floor((86400 - 1 - prefix_sum_i) / 86400) + something
    
    # Actually, T must be in [0, 86400), so for each i, we need:
    # 0 <= 14400 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400 <= 86400 and 0 <= 25200 - prefix_sum_i + k*86400 <= 86400
    
    # This is too complex for N=10^5.
    
    # Alternative insight: the maximum number of wake-ups in 4-7 AM is independent of T's specific value, and we can use a different approach.
    
    # For each wake-up i, the wake-up time is (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400.
    # This time is in [14400, 25200] if and only if there exists integer k such that:
    # 14400 + k*86400 <= T + prefix_sum_i < 25200 + k*86400
    
    # Rearranging: 14400 + k*86400 - prefix_sum_i <= T < 25200 + k*86400 - prefix_sum_i
    
    # This gives a range of valid T for wake-up i with integer k.
    
    # We want to find T that maximizes the number of i for which there exists k such that T is in that range.
    
    # For each i, we have a set of intervals for T.
    # We want to find the value of T that is in the most such intervals.
    
    # Since the intervals are periodic, we can consider T in [0, 86400) and only consider k such that the interval overlaps [0, 86400).
    
    # For each i, we find all k such that the interval [14400 + k*86400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 + k*86400 - prefix_sum_i] overlaps [0, 86400).
    
    # The number of k values is bounded because prefix_sum_i is at most 10^5 * 10^5 = 10^10, and 86400 is about 8.64e4, so k is at most about 10^5, which is acceptable.
    
    # But for each i, we might need to iterate over many k values, and for N=10^5, this is 10^10 operations which is too slow.
    
    # I need a more efficient approach.
    
    # Let me look for a simpler solution.
    
    # After research and thinking, I recall that in such cyclic problems, we can use the fact that the maximum number of overlapping events can be found by considering the boundaries.
    
    # For each wake-up i, the valid T values are in intervals of the form [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k]
    
    # We can generate events at the boundaries of these intervals for a reasonable range of k.
    
    # The bounds for k: 
    # For a given i, the interval [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k] must intersect [0, 86400)
    # So:
    # 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k < 86400
    # or 
    # 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k < 86400 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k >= 0
    
    # Actually, it's easier to generate events for k such that the interval has positive length and overlaps [0, 86400).
    
    # Since the length is 10800, and period is 86400, for each i, there are at most 2 intervals that overlap [0, 86400) (one in the first cycle, one in the second, etc.).
    
    # Actually, for each i and each k, there is at most one interval, and the number of k values is roughly floor((86400 - 1) / 86400) = 1, plus a little.
    
    # Let's calculate the bounds for k:
    # The condition for the interval to intersect [0, 86400) is:
    # max(0, -14400 + prefix_sum_i) <= (25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k) - (14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k) = 10800
    # And the interval must overlap [0, 86400).
    
    # The interval [low, high] = [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k] overlaps [0, 86400) if:
    # low < 86400 and high > 0
    # i.e., 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k < 86400 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400*k > 0
    # So:
    # k < (86400 - 14400 + prefix_sum_i) / 86400 = (72000 + prefix_sum_i) / 86400
    # k > ( -25200 + prefix_sum_i ) / 86400
    
    # Since prefix_sum_i can be large, k can be large or small.
    
    # But the range of k is from ceil((prefix_sum_i - 25200) / 86400) to floor((86400 + 14400 - prefix_sum_i) / 86400)
    
    # This is complex. Given the complexity, and that the intended solution might be simpler, let me try to find a different approach.
    
    # After re-examining, I think the intended solution is to consider that for each wake-up, the condition is on the time of day, and we can use a sweep line over the boundaries of the wake-up times.
    
    # The wake-up times are at T + prefix_sum_i (mod 86400)
    # We want these to be in [14400, 25200]
    
    # For each i, the wake-up time is in [14400, 25200] if (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400 in [14400, 25200]
    # This is equivalent to T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] mod 86400
    
    # So we can generate events at the boundaries of these intervals.
    
    # For each i, we have an interval [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] mod 86400
    # This can be split into at most two or three intervals in [0, 86400)
    
    # Specifically, if 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 0, then it wraps around
    # If 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i <= 86400, then it's in [0,86400)
    # If 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 0, then it's split at 0
    
    # So for each i, we can generate up to 2 events in [0,86400)
    
    # Let's do that.
    
    pass

# Given the complexity and that I'm spending too much time, I'll implement the solution with event-based approach for the interval in [0,86400)

events = []

# Calculate prefix sums
prefix_sums = []
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b = pairs[i]
    current += a
    prefix_sums.append(current)

# For each wake-up i, find the valid T ranges in [0, 86400)
for i in range(n):
    prefix_sum_i = prefix_sums[i]
    
    # We want (T + prefix_sum_i) mod 86400 in [14400, 25200]
    # This is equivalent to T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] modulo 86400
    
    # Case 1: if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 86400
    if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 86400:
        # T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i]
        events.append((14400 - prefix_sum_i, 1))
        events.append((25200 - prefix_sum_i, -1))
    
    # Case 2: if 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 0
    elif 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 0:
        # T in [0, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] and [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 86400)
        events.append((0, 1))
        events.append((25200 - prefix_sum_i, -1))
        events.append((14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 1))
        events.append((86400, -1))  # Note: 86400 is exclusive, so event at end
        
    # Case 3: if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400
    # This is not possible because 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 86400 after 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0
    # because if 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400, then 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400 - 10800 = 75600, which is > 86400
    # so if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0, then 25200 - prefix_sum_i <= 10800 < 86400
    # So case 3 doesn't occur.
    
    # Case 4: if 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 0
    elif 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 0:
        # T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400]
        # and since 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 < 86400 only if 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0 (true) 
        # and 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 >= 0 (true), and similarly for the upper bound
        # so T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400] 
        # but this interval might be > 86400
        # if 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 >= 86400, then T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 86400)
        # and if 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 < 86400, then T in [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400]
        
        # Actually, 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 < 86400 because 14400 - prefix_sum_i < 0
        # and 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400 > 86400 because 25200 - prefix_sum_i > 0? No, we have 25200 - prefix_sum_i < 0
        
        # So the entire interval [14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400, 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400] might be > 86400
        
        # We want T in [0, 86400), so we take intersection
        low = 14400 - prefix_sum_i + 86400
        high = 25200 - prefix_sum_i + 86400
        if low < 0:
            low = 0
        if high > 86400:
            high = 86400
        if low < high:
            events.append((low, 1))
            events.append((high, -1))
    
    # Case 5: if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0 and 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400
    # As argued, this is impossible because if 25200 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400, then 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 86400 - 10800 = 75600, which is > 86400, 
    # so if 14400 - prefix_sum_i >= 0, then 25200 - prefix_sum_i <= 10800 < 86400
    # so this case doesn't occur.

# After sorting events, find maximum count
events.sort()
max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)
```

I see that the above approach might have issues with the boundaries. Let me simplify and implement a cleaner version that should work for the given constraints.

```python
n = int(input())
pairs = []
prefix_sums = []

current_sum = 0
for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    current_sum += a
    pairs.append((a, b))
    prefix_sums.append(current_sum)

events = []

# For each wake-up i, the wake-up time is (T + prefix_sums[i]) mod 86400
# We want this to be in [14400, 25200]
# This means T must be in [14400 - prefix_sums[i], 25200 - prefix_sums[i]] mod 86400

for i in range(n):
    prefix_sum_i = prefix_sums[i]
    
    # The valid values for T are in the interval [14400 - prefix_sum_i, 25200 - prefix_sum_i] modulo 86400
    # We need to split this into intervals within [0, 86400)
    
    low = 14400 - prefix_sum_i
    high = 25200 - prefix_sum_i
    
    # Case 1: the interval is fully in [0, 86400)
    if low >= 0 and high < 86400:
        events.append((low, 1))
        events.append((high, -1))
    
    # Case 2: the interval wraps around (low < 0 and high >= 0)
    elif low < 0 and high >= 0:
        # T in [0, high] and [low + 86400, 86400)
        events.append((0, 1))
        events.append((high, -1))
        events.append((low + 86400, 1))
        events.append((86400, -1))
    
    # Case 3: the interval is fully negative (low < 0 and high < 0)
    elif low < 0 and high < 0:
        # T in [low + 86400, high + 86400] intersect [0, 86400)
        start = low + 86400
        end = high + 86400
        
        # Intersect with [0, 86400)
        if start < 0:
            start = 0
        if end > 86400:
            end = 86400
            
        if start < end:
            events.append((start, 1))
            events.append((end, -1))

# Sort events by time
events.sort()

max_count = 0
current_count = 0

for time, delta in events:
    current_count += delta
    max_count = max(max_count, current_count)

print(max_count)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

Print the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Examples

Input

3
28800 57600
28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2


Input

10
28800 57600
4800 9600
6000 1200
600 600
300 600
5400 600
6000 5760
6760 2880
6000 12000
9000 600


Output

5

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Takahashi has a sequence of sleep/awake cycles: a₁, b₁, a₂, b₂, ..., aₙ, bₙ
2. Each cycle: sleep for aᵢ seconds, then awake for bᵢ seconds
3. We need to find how many times he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM (inclusive)
4. The key insight: we don't know the starting time T, so we can choose T to maximize the number of early wake-ups
5. A day has 86400 seconds, and 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds

## Key Insights

1. Wake-up times occur at: T + sum of all preceding sleep times + (sleep time of previous cycle)
2. For each wake-up time, we need to check if it falls within [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]
3. Since we can choose T freely, we can shift the entire timeline to maximize early wake-ups
4. A wake-up occurs at time t (relative to T) when we wake up during cycle i, at time: T + sum of all aⱼ for j < i + aᵢ

## Strategy

1. Calculate the cumulative time from the start to each wake-up point
2. For each wake-up time, determine the time offset from start
3. Since we can shift T by any amount modulo 86400, we want to find the shift that maximizes wake-ups in [0, 10800) seconds
4. The wake-up times relative to T are: T + c₁, T + c₂, ..., T + cₙ where cᵢ is the cumulative time to wake up at cycle i
5. For each wake-up time, we get a condition on T: (T + cᵢ) mod 86400 ∈ [0, 10800)

Actually, I think the maximum possible count is the number of wake-up times cᵢ such that cᵢ ∈ [0, 10800] or cᵢ ∈ [86400 - 10800, 86400] (i.e., cᵢ ∈ [75600, 86400]).

Wait, that's not correct either.

```python

# end_c = (10800 - c) % 86400

# Since 10800 - c_mod < 86400 - c_mod, end_c < start_c when 10800 - c_mod < 86400 - c_mod, which is always true.

# So when c_mod > 10800, end_c < 0, so end_c = 10800 - c_mod + 86400 > 0.

# When c_mod <= 10800, end_c = 10800 - c_mod >= 0, and since 10800 - c_mod < 86400 - c_mod, end_c < start_c.

# This means that when c_mod > 10800, the interval is [86400 - c_mod, 10800 - c_mod + 86400]
# When c_mod <= 10800, the interval is empty or [86400 - c_mod, 10800 - c_mod] with 10800 - c_mod < 86400 - c_mod, so it's empty if 10800 - c_mod < 0.

# I think I have a mistake.

# This is equivalent to T in [(-c) mod 86400, (10800 - c) mod 86400]

# In modular arithmetic:
# (T + c) mod 86400 in [0, 10800]
# implies T in [(-c) mod 86400, (10800 - c) mod 86400]

# The length of this interval is:
# if c <= 10800: (10800 - c) - (-c) = 10800 (mod 86400)
# if c > 10800: (10800 - c + 86400) - (86400 - c) = 10800 (mod 86400)

# So every such interval has length 10800.

# And the interval is non-empty as long as 10800 > 0.

# For the interval to be valid, we need:
# when c <= 10800: T in [86400 - c, 10800 - c] - but this is impossible since 86400 - c > 10800 - c.
# I think I have a fundamental error.

# Let me resolve this mathematically:
# (T + c) mod 86400 in [0, 10800]
# means there exists integer k such that 0 <= T + c - 86400k <= 10800
# so 86400k <= T + c <= 86400k + 10800
# so T in [86400k - c, 86400k + 10800 - c]

# For each wake-up, we have such an interval for some k.

# For T in [0, 86400), we only need to consider k=0 and k=1.

# For k=0: T in [-c, 10800 - c]
# For k=1: T in [86400 - c, 86400 + 10800 - c] = [86400 - c, 97200 - c]

# Since T in [0, 86400), the valid intervals are:
# - From k=0: T in [max(0, -c), min(86400, 10800 - c)] if 10800 - c >= 0
# - From k=1: T in [max(0, 86400 - c), min(86400, 97200 - c)] but 97200 - c > 86400 when c < 97200, and since c <= 86400, 97200 - c > 86400.

# So from k=1: T in [86400 - c, 86400] if 86400 - c >= 0, i.e., c <= 86400

# So for each wake-up at time c, the valid T in [0, 86400) are:
# - If c <= 10800: T in [0, 10800 - c]  (from k=0)
# - If c > 10800: T in [86400 - c, 86400]  (from k=1)

# This makes sense:
# - If c <= 10800, then T + c in [0, 10800] if T in [0, 10800 - c]
# - If c > 10800, then T + c in [86400, 86400 + 10800] if T in [86400 - c, 86400]

# So for each wake-up, the valid T-intervals are:
# - If c <= 10800: [0, 10800 - c]
# - If c > 10800: [86400 - c, 86400]

# Now we want to find a point T in [0, 86400) that maximizes the number of wake-ups that are early.

# We can do this by checking all endpoints and the boundaries.

# The candidate T values are:
# - 0
# - 10800 - c for each c <= 10800
# - 86400 - c for each c > 10800
# - 86400 (which is 0)

# But since we're only interested in T in [0, 86400), we can consider:
# - 0
# - 10800 - c for each c <= 10800
# - 86400 - c for each c > 10800

# We'll evaluate the number of wake-ups that would be early at each of these candidate T values.

# However, there might be a better way. Since the intervals are disjoint or overlapping in a simple way, we can just count for each T.

# Given the constraints (N up to 10^5), we can't iterate over all T values.

# Instead, we can note that a wake-up at time c will be early if T is in:
# - [0, 10800 - c] for c <= 10800
# - [86400 - c, 86400] for c > 10800

# We want to find the T that maximizes the count.

# The function is piecewise constant and changes only at the breakpoints.

# So we can collect all breakpoints and evaluate at each.

# Breakpoints are:
# - 0
# - 10800 - c for each c <= 10800
# - 86400 - c for each c > 10800

# Then evaluate count at each breakpoint.

# But also need to check if there are any values between breakpoints that might be better.

# Actually, between any two consecutive breakpoints, the count is constant, so we only need to check at breakpoints.

# Implementation:

```python
N = int(input())
wake_up_times = []

cumulative = 0
for _ in range(N):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    # Wake up after sleeping a seconds in this cycle
    wake_up_times.append(cumulative + a)
    cumulative += a + b

# We will find candidate T values and evaluate for each
candidates = set()

# Add 0
candidates.add(0)

# For each wake-up time c
for c in wake_up_times:
    c_mod = c % 86400
    
    if c_mod <= 10800:
        # T in [0, 10800 - c_mod]
        candidates.add(10800 - c_mod)
    else:
        # T in [86400 - c_mod, 86400]
        candidates.add(86400 - c_mod)

# Also add 86400 (which is 0)
candidates.add(86400)

# Evaluate the number of early wake-ups for each candidate T
max_count = 0

for T in candidates:
    T = T % 86400
    count = 0
    for c in wake_up_times:
        c_mod = c % 86400
        if c_mod <= 10800:
            if T <= 10800 - c_mod:
                count += 1
        else:
            if T >= 86400 - c_mod:
                count += 1
    max_count = max(max_count, count)

print(max_count)
```

```python
N = int(input())

    cumulative += a + b

candidates = set()

# Add 0
candidates.add(0)

# For each wake-up time c (actual time in seconds from start)
for c in wake_up_times:
    c_mod = c % 86400
    
    if c_mod <= 10800:
        # T should be in [0, 10800 - c_mod]
        # This is because (T + c_mod) mod 86400 in [0, 10800]
        # when T in [0, 10800 - c_mod]
        candidates.add(10800 - c_mod)
    else:
        # T should be in [86400 - c_mod, 86400]
        # because (T + c_mod) mod 86400 in [0, 10800]
        # when T in [86400 - c_mod, 86400]
        candidates.add(86400 - c_mod)

# Also add 86400 (which is 0)
candidates.add(86400)

    count = 0

        c_mod = c % 86400
        if c_mod <= 10800:
            # (T + c_mod) mod 86400 = T + c_mod (since T + c_mod < 10800 + 10800 = 21600 < 86400)
            if T <= 10800 - c_mod:
                count += 1
        else:
            # (T + c_mod) mod 86400 = T + c_mod - 86400
            # This is in [0, 10800] if 0 <= T + c_mod - 86400 <= 10800
            # which means T >= 86400 - c_mod and T <= 97200 - c_mod
            # But since T < 86400 and c_mod > 10800, 97200 - c_mod < 86400
            # and T >= 86400 - c_mod
            if T >= 86400 - c_mod:
                count += 1
    max_count = max(max_count, count)

print(max_count)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:


N
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

Print the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Examples

Input

3
28800 57600
28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2

10
28800 57600
4800 9600
6000 1200
600 600
300 600
5400 600
6000 5760
6760 2880
6000 12000
9000 600


Output

5

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Takahashi has a sequence of sleeping and awake periods: a₁, b₁, a₂, b₂, ..., aₙ, bₙ
2. He starts at some unknown time T
3. We need to find the maximum number of times he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM (inclusive)
4. A day has 86400 seconds, and 4:00 AM to 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds
5. Each wake-up event is counted independently

## Key Insights

1. Since we don't know the starting time T, we can shift the entire timeline by any amount
2. The wake-up times occur at: T + (a₁ + b₁) + (a₂ + b₂) + ... + (aᵢ + bᵢ) for each i
3. For each wake-up event at time t, we want to know if t falls in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM] = [10800, 205200] seconds from midnight (or any reference point)
4. Since we can shift T arbitrarily, we need to find the optimal shift that maximizes wake-ups in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM]

## Approach

1. Calculate the time of each wake-up event relative to the start T
2. A wake-up at position i happens at time: T + sum of all (a_j + b_j) for j=1 to i
3. For a wake-up at time T + Sᵢ to fall in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM], we need: 10800 ≤ T + Sᵢ ≤ 205200 (mod 86400)
4. This gives us: 10800 - Sᵢ ≤ T ≤ 205200 - Sᵢ (mod 86400)

5. For each wake-up event i, we want to know if there exists a T such that T + Sᵢ ∈ [10800, 205200] (mod 86400)
6. The optimal T will be such that as many wake-up events as possible fall in the 4-7 AM window

7. The key insight is that we can use modular arithmetic. For each wake-up time Sᵢ relative to start, we want to know if (T + Sᵢ) mod 86400 ∈ [10800, 205200].

8. Since we can choose T, for wake-up i, we want T ∈ [10800 - Sᵢ, 205200 - Sᵢ] (mod 86400)

9. We can transform this to: T mod 86400 ∈ [10800 - Sᵢ, 205200 - Sᵢ] (mod 86400)

10. Actually, simpler approach: For each wake-up time, we can calculate when it would occur in the 4-7 AM window. Since we can shift T, we want to maximize the number of wake-ups that fall in the range [10800, 205200] (mod 86400).

11. For each wake-up i at time Sᵢ, the condition is: (T + Sᵢ) mod 86400 ∈ [10800, 205200]

12. This means T mod 86400 ∈ [(10800 - Sᵢ) mod 86400, (205200 - Sᵢ) mod 86400]

13. We need to find T that maximizes the count of wake-ups in this range.

14. Instead of checking all possible T, we can consider that the maximum will occur when T is chosen optimally. The optimal T will make as many wake-ups as possible fall in [10800, 205200].

15. For each wake-up, we can determine the range of T that makes it fall in 4-7 AM. Then we need to find the T value that covers the maximum number of such ranges.

16. But there's a simpler insight: since we're working modulo 86400, we can think about it this way - we want to find a shift T such that as many wake-up times as possible fall in the 4-7 AM window.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a_b = []
    for _ in range(n):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        a_b.append((a, b))
    
    # Calculate prefix sums of (a_i + b_i)
    prefix_sum = [0]
    for i in range(n):
        prefix_sum.append(prefix_sum[-1] + a_b[i][0] + a_b[i][1])
    
    # The wake-up times are at positions: prefix_sum[1], prefix_sum[2], ..., prefix_sum[n]
    # We want to find T such that (T + prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 is in [10800, 205200]
    
    # For each wake-up i, the condition is:
    # (T + prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 >= 10800 and (T + prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 <= 205200
    
    # This means T mod 86400 >= (10800 - prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 and T mod 86400 <= (205200 - prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400
    
    # We need to find T such that the maximum number of these conditions are satisfied
    
    wake_up_ranges = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        s = prefix_sum[i]
        # We want T such that (T + s) mod 86400 is in [10800, 205200]
        # This means T mod 86400 must be in [10800 - s, 205200 - s] (mod 86400)
        
        # Normalize the range
        low = (10800 - s) % 86400
        high = (205200 - s) % 86400
        
        # If high < low, it means the range wraps around
        if high < low:
            wake_up_ranges.append((low, 86400))
            wake_up_ranges.append((0, high))
        else:
            wake_up_ranges.append((low, high))
    
    # Now we need to find the value of T mod 86400 that maximizes the number of ranges it falls into
    # Since the ranges are on a circular timeline, we can check the endpoints of each range
    
    # The optimal T will be at one of the endpoints of the ranges or between them
    # But since each range is contiguous, we can just check for each possible "shift"
    
    # Actually, we can simplify: we want to maximize the count of wake-ups in [10800, 205200]
    # For a given T, wake-up i is in 4-7 AM if (T + prefix_sum[i]) % 86400 is in [10800, 205200]
    
    # We can try to find the optimal T by considering the boundaries
    
    # The maximum occurs when T is chosen such that as many wake-ups as possible fall in the interval
    # Since the total sequence is periodic, we can check T values at key points
    
    # The key points are: -prefix_sum[i] mod 86400 for each i (which gives the shift that makes wake-up i occur at 0)
    # But actually, we can check the boundaries of the optimal ranges
    
    max_count = 0
    
    # Since the function is piecewise constant, the maximum will occur at one of the endpoints
    # of the ranges we created
    endpoints = set()
    
    # Add endpoints from each range
    for low, high in wake_up_ranges:
        endpoints.add(low)
        endpoints.add(high)
    
    # Also consider the endpoints of the intervals we have
    # We should also consider 0 and 86400 (which are the same)
    
    # Check each potential T value
    for t in endpoints:
        count = 0
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            wake_up_time = (t + prefix_sum[i]) % 86400
            if 10800 <= wake_up_time <= 205200:
                count += 1
        max_count = max(max_count, count)
    
    # Also check a few more points to be safe
    # But given the nature of the problem, endpoints should suffice
    
    return max_count

print(solve())
```

Wait, I think there's a simpler approach. Let me reconsider.

Actually, the key insight is that we're looking for the maximum number of wake-ups that can fall in the 4-7 AM window with some optimal starting time T.

For each wake-up i, we can calculate the required shift in T: we need T ≡ (10800 - prefix_sum[i]) (mod 86400) to make it occur at 4:00 AM, and T ≡ (205200 - prefix_sum[i]) (mod 86400) to make it occur at 7:00 AM.

But since we want to maximize the count, and the shifts are continuous, we can think about it differently.

```python
n = int(input())
a_b = []

    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    a_b.append((a, b))

# Calculate prefix sums of (a_i + b_i)

    prefix_sum.append(prefix_sum[-1] + a_b[i][0] + a_b[i][1])

# But we can't try all 86400 values

# Instead, we only need to check the critical points where a wake-up might enter or exit the 4-7 AM window
# These occur when (T + prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 = 10800 or (T + prefix_sum[i]) mod 86400 = 205200

# The critical T values are: (10800 - prefix_sum[i]) % 86400 and (205200 - prefix_sum[i]) % 86400

critical_points = set()
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    s = prefix_sum[i]
    t1 = (10800 - s) % 86400
    t2 = (205200 - s) % 86400
    critical_points.add(t1)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi recorded his daily life for the last few days as a integer sequence of length 2N, as follows:

* a_1, b_1, a_2, b_2, ... , a_N, b_N



This means that, starting from a certain time T, he was:

* sleeping for exactly a_1 seconds
* then awake for exactly b_1 seconds
* then sleeping for exactly a_2 seconds
* :
* then sleeping for exactly a_N seconds
* then awake for exactly b_N seconds



In this record, he waked up N times.

Takahashi is wondering how many times he waked up early during the recorded period.

Here, he is said to wake up early if he wakes up between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM, inclusive.

If he wakes up more than once during this period, each of these awakenings is counted as waking up early.

Unfortunately, he forgot the time T.

Find the maximum possible number of times he waked up early during the recorded period.

For your information, a day consists of 86400 seconds, and the length of the period between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM is 10800 seconds.

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
* 1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^5
* a_i and b_i are integers.

Input

N

a_2 b_2
:
a_N b_N


Output

3
28800 57600
28800 57600
57600 28800


Output

2

6760 2880

9000 600

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N sleep-awake cycles: (a₁,b₁), (a₂,b₂), ..., (aₙ,bₙ)
2. Each cycle is: sleep for aᵢ seconds, then awake for bᵢ seconds
3. We need to find the maximum number of times Takahashi wakes up early (between 4:00 AM and 7:00 AM inclusive)
4. The day has 86400 seconds (24 hours)
5. The early period (4:00 AM to 7:00 AM) has 10800 seconds

## Key Insight

Since we don't know the starting time T, we can choose T to maximize the count of early wake-ups. The wake-up times depend on T plus the cumulative duration of sleep cycles.

For each wake-up event (after sleeping aᵢ), the wake-up time is:
- T + sum of all aⱼ for j ≤ i (where j < i, aⱼ is the sleep duration before the current cycle)

We need to check if this wake-up time falls within [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM].

## Solution Strategy

1. Calculate the cumulative sleep time before each wake-up
2. For each wake-up time (T + cumulative_sleep_time), determine if it falls in the early period
3. Since we can choose T optimally, we need to find which choices of T maximize the count

The key insight is that we can think of this as: we have N wake-up times, and we want to find how many of them can land in the early period [0, 10800) when we adjust T.

Actually, let's think differently: since we can choose T freely, we can shift all wake-up times by T. So we want to find the maximum number of wake-up times that can fall in [4:00 AM, 7:00 AM] after adding T.

For a wake-up time to be early, we need:
- 4:00 AM ≤ (T + cumulative_sleep_time) ≤ 7:00 AM
- 0 ≤ (T + cumulative_sleep_time) mod 86400 ≤ 10800

Since we can choose T arbitrarily, we need to check for each wake-up time if it's possible for it to fall in the early period.

For a wake-up time at offset `offset` from T, it falls in early period if:
- (T + offset) mod 86400 is in [0, 10800]

This is equivalent to T being in [-(offset) mod 86400, (10800 - offset) mod 86400]

Since we want the maximum count, we can use a sliding window or check if multiple events can occur simultaneously.

Actually, simpler approach:
- Calculate all wake-up times relative to T
- The condition for wake-up i to be early is: (T + cum_sum[i]) mod 86400 ∈ [0, 10800]
- This means T mod 86400 ∈ [(0 - cum_sum[i]) mod 86400, (10800 - cum_sum[i]) mod 86400]

For each wake-up i, we have an interval of valid T values. We want to know how many of these intervals can overlap.

But since we're looking for maximum possible count, we can simply check if any choice of T can make multiple wake-ups fall in the early period.

The maximum count is simply the number of wake-up events in the early period for some choice of T. Since we can shift T, we can try to align multiple wake-ups to the early period.
